It’s hard to believe everyone in the CNC lab isn’t noticing that before my work.\n\nDoctor: Has your concern about the left side of your face looking crooked changed in intensity over time?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it’s gotten worse, I think. But I came in because I can’t stop thinking about it. In every ID photo or on the online class camera, the left side of my face looks off to me—my nose and jaw look crooked. And even when someone says it looks normal, I still feel like people in the CNC lab notice that before they notice my work.\n\nDoctor: Is your concern about the left side of your face affecting your ability to do things you want or need to do?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it is. At first I’d just notice it in pictures sometimes, but now it’s like I can’t stop thinking about the left side of my face looking off. The ID photos, the class camera… it all makes my nose and jaw look crooked to me, and then I keep thinking people in the CNC lab are seeing that before anything else.\n\nDoctor: Do you think there's any chance that your belief about the left side of your face being crooked might not be based in reality?\n\nPatient: I guess there’s a chance… because people keep telling me they don’t see it. But I came in because I can’t stop thinking about it. In every ID photo or on the online class camera, the left side of my face looks off to me—my nose and jaw look crooked. And even when someone says it looks normal, I still feel like people in the CNC lab notice that before they notice my work.\n\nDoctor: Is your concern about the left side of your face affecting your ability to do things you want or need to do?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it is. I’ve missed some CNC labs and shop shifts because I just couldn’t make myself leave. My faculty mentor said my work placement might be in trouble if it keeps happening.\n\nAnd… I stopped playing soccer and volunteering. I just don’t want people looking at me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think other people can see the crookedness in your face, even if they don't say anything about it?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. Even if they don’t say anything, I feel like they can see it. Like in the CNC lab, I keep thinking they notice my face being off before they notice anything I’m doing.\n\nDoctor: Do you think it's possible that others are intentionally not telling you about your face being crooked, even if they've noticed it?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think that sometimes. Like maybe they notice it but don’t want to be rude or make me feel worse. Even when people say they don’t see it, I still can’t stop thinking the left side looks off—my nose, my jaw… especially in photos or on camera. And in the CNC lab, it feels like they’re seeing that before anything else.\n\nDoctor: Do you think it's possible that your belief about your face being crooked is not true, even if it feels that way to you?\n\nPatient: I mean… I guess it’s possible. Like in the CNC lab, I keep thinking they notice my face being off before they notice anything I’m doing.\n\nDoctor: Do you think it's possible that others are intentionally not telling you about your face being crooked, even if they've noticed it?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think that sometimes. Like maybe they notice it but don’t want to be rude or make me feel worse. Even when people say they don’t see it, I still can’t stop thinking the left side looks off—my nose, my jaw… especially in photos or on camera. And in the CNC lab, it feels like they’re seeing that before anything else.\n\nDoctor: Do you think it's possible that your belief about your face being crooked is not true, even if it feels that way to you?\n\nPatient: I mean… I guess it’s possible. People keep saying they don’t see it.\n\nBut it doesn’t feel possible when I’m looking at a photo or my camera for class. I just see the left side—my nose, my jaw—looking crooked, and then I can’t stop thinking everyone in the CNC lab notices that before anything I’m doing.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your belief about your face being crooked would change if you were shown a professional, neutral assessment of your appearance, such as from a plastic surgeon?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… maybe for a little bit, but I think I’d still keep seeing it.\n\nLike, even when people tell me they don’t notice anything, I look at my ID photo or the camera for class and the left side just looks wrong to me—my nose, my jaw. Then I start thinking everyone in the CNC lab sees that before they see my work.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing.     "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n13\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF45.2, F22\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Um… I guess I’m here because I can’t stop thinking about my face. Mostly the left side. It looks… off to me, like my nose and jaw are crooked, especially in ID photos or on the camera for online class.\n\nPeople say they don’t notice it, but I still feel like everyone in the CNC lab sees that first, before anything I’m doing.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your face is actually crooked, or is it just that you feel like it looks that way to others?\n\nPatient: I mean… I think it is crooked. Or at least it looks that way to me. The left side just looks off, like my nose and jaw don’t line up right, especially in photos or on camera. Even when people say they don’t see it, I still feel like they do—like people in the lab notice that before anything else.\n\nDoctor: Is it only your face that you're concerned about, or are there other parts of your body that you worry about as well?\n\nPatient: It’s mostly my face. The left side, specifically… like my nose and jaw look crooked to me. I notice it in ID photos or on the camera for class, and then I can’t really stop thinking about it. Even in the CNC lab, I feel like people see that before they see my work.\n\nDoctor: Do you think it's possible that your face might actually look normal, but you just can't see it that way?\n\nPatient: I… I guess it’s possible. People keep saying they don’t see it. But when I look at ID photos or the class camera, the left side just looks wrong to me—like my nose and jaw are crooked. And then in the CNC lab I feel like that’s what everyone notices first.\n\nDoctor: Do you think it's possible for you to be mistaken about your face being crooked?\n\nPatient: I mean… maybe. People tell me they don’t see it, so I guess I could be wrong. But when I see my ID photo or the online class camera, the left side just looks off—like my nose and jaw are crooked. It’s hard to believe everyone in the CNC lab isn’t noticing that before my work.\n\nDoctor: Has your concern about the left side of your face looking crooked changed in intensity over time?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it’s gotten worse, I think. At first I’d just notice it in pictures sometimes, but now it’s like I can’t stop thinking about the left side of my face looking off. The ID photos, the class camera… it all makes my nose and jaw look crooked to me, and then I keep thinking people in the CNC lab are seeing that before anything else.\n\nDoctor: Do you think there's any chance that your belief about the left side of your face being crooked might not be based in reality?\n\nPatient: I guess there’s a chance… because people keep telling me they don’t see it. But I came in because I can’t stop thinking about it. In every ID photo or on the online class camera, the left side of my face looks off to me—my nose and jaw look crooked. And even when someone says it looks normal, I still feel like people in the CNC lab notice that before they notice my work.\n\nDoctor: Is your concern about the left side of your face affecting your ability to do things you want or need to do?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it is. I’ve missed some CNC labs and shop shifts because I just couldn’t make myself leave. My faculty mentor said my work placement might be in trouble if it keeps happening.\n\nAnd… I stopped playing soccer and volunteering. I just don’t want people looking at me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think other people can see the crookedness in your face, even if they don't say anything about it?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. Even if they don’t say anything, I feel like they can see it. Like in the CNC lab, I keep thinking they notice my face being off before they notice anything I’m doing.\n\nDoctor: Do you think it's possible that others are intentionally not telling you about your face being crooked, even if they've noticed it?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think that sometimes. And in the CNC lab, it feels like they’re seeing that before anything else.\n\nDoctor: Do you think it's possible that your belief about your face being crooked is not true, even if it feels that way to you?\n\nPatient: I mean… I guess it’s possible. People keep saying they don’t see it.\n\nBut it doesn’t feel possible when I’m looking at a photo or my camera for class. I just see the left side—my nose, my jaw—looking crooked, and then I can’t stop thinking everyone in the CNC lab notices that before anything I’m doing.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your belief about your face being crooked would change if you were shown a professional, neutral assessment of your appearance, such as from a plastic surgeon?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… maybe for a little bit, but I think I’d still keep seeing it.\n\nLike, even when people tell me they don’t notice anything, I look at my ID photo or the camera for class and the left side just looks wrong to me—my nose, my jaw. Then I start thinking everyone in the CNC lab sees that before they see my work.\n\nDoctor: Do you think there's anything that could convince you that your face isn't actually crooked?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know. Maybe if I could stop seeing it in photos or on camera, but… I came in because I can’t stop thinking the left side of my face looks off. My nose, my jaw—especially in my ID photo or online class camera.\n\nEven when people say they don’t see it, I still feel like everyone in the CNC lab notices that first, before my work. So it’s hard to imagine what would convince me.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Um… I guess I’m here because I can’t stop thinking about my face. Mostly the left side. It looks… off to me, like my nose and jaw are crooked, especially in ID photos or on the camera for online class.\n\nPeople say they don’t notice it, but I still feel like everyone in the CNC lab sees that first, before anything I’m doing.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your face is actually crooked, or is it just that you feel like it looks that way to others?\n\nPatient: I mean… I think it is crooked. Or at least it looks that way to me. The left side just looks off, like my nose and jaw don’t line up right, especially in photos or on camera. Even when people say they don’t see it, I still feel like they do—like people in the lab notice that before anything else.\n\nDoctor: Is it only your face that you're concerned about, or are there other parts of your body that you worry about as well?\n\nPatient: It’s mostly my face. The left side, specifically… like my nose and jaw look crooked to me. I notice it in ID photos or on the camera for class, and then I can’t really stop thinking about it. Even in the CNC lab, I feel like people see that before they see my work.\n\nDoctor: Do you think it's possible that your face might actually look normal, but you just can't see it that way?\n\nPatient: I… I guess it’s possible. People keep saying they don’t see it. But when I look at ID photos or the class camera, the left side just looks wrong to me—like my nose and jaw are crooked. And then in the CNC lab I feel like that’s what everyone notices first.\n\nDoctor: Do you think it's possible for you to be mistaken about your face being crooked?\n\nPatient: I mean… maybe. People tell me they don’t see it, so I guess I could be wrong. But when I see my ID photo or the online class camera, the left side just looks off—like my nose and jaw are crooked. It’s hard to believe everyone in the CNC lab isn’t noticing that before my work.\n\nDoctor: Has your concern about the left side of your face looking crooked changed in intensity over time?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it’s gotten worse, I think. At first I’d just notice it in pictures sometimes, but now it’s like I can’t stop thinking about the left side of my face looking off. The ID photos, the class camera… it all makes my nose and jaw look crooked to me, and then I keep thinking people in the CNC lab are seeing that before anything else.\n\nDoctor: Do you think there's any chance that your belief about the left side of your face being crooked might not be based in reality?\n\nPatient: I guess there’s a chance… because people keep telling me they don’t see it. But I came in because I can’t stop thinking about it. In every ID photo or on the online class camera, the left side of my face looks off to me—my nose and jaw look crooked. And even when someone says it looks normal, I still feel like people in the CNC lab notice that before they notice my work.\n\nDoctor: Is your concern about the left side of your face affecting your ability to do things you want or need to do?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it is. I’ve missed some CNC labs and shop shifts because I just couldn’t make myself leave. My faculty mentor said my work placement might be in trouble if it keeps happening.\n\nAnd… I stopped playing soccer and volunteering. I just don’t want people looking at me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think other people can see the crookedness in your face, even if they don't say anything about it?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. Even if they don’t say anything, I feel like they can see it. Like in the CNC lab, I keep thinking they notice my face being off before they notice anything I’m doing.\n\nDoctor: Do you think it's possible that others are intentionally not telling you about your face being crooked, even if they've noticed it?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think that sometimes. Like maybe they notice it but don’t want to be rude or make me feel worse. Even when people say they don’t see it, I still can’t stop thinking the left side looks off—my nose, my jaw… especially in photos or on camera. I just see the left side—my nose, my jaw—looking crooked, and then I can’t stop thinking everyone in the CNC lab notices that before anything I’m doing.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your belief about your face being crooked would change if you were shown a professional, neutral assessment of your appearance, such as from a plastic surgeon?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… maybe for a little bit, but I think I’d still keep seeing it.\n\nLike, even when people tell me they don’t notice anything, I look at my ID photo or the camera for class and the left side just looks wrong to me—my nose, my jaw. Then I start thinking everyone in the CNC lab sees that before they see my work.\n\nDoctor: Do you think there's anything that could convince you that your face isn't actually crooked?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know. Maybe if I could stop seeing it in photos or on camera, but… I came in because I can’t stop thinking the left side of my face looks off. My nose, my jaw—especially in my ID photo or online class camera.\n\nEven when people say they don’t see it, I still feel like everyone in the CNC lab notices that first, before my work. So it’s hard to imagine what would convince me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your belief about your face being crooked is based on your own perception or on something that you think is objectively true?\n\nPatient: I guess it’s mostly my own perception… but it feels objective when I’m looking at it.\n\nLike in my ID photo or on the class camera, the left side of my face just looks off—my nose and jaw. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."

I mean… part of me knows it could be mistaken, I guess. But when I see it in my ID photo or on the class camera, it feels like a fact. It looks… off to me, like my nose and jaw are crooked, especially in ID photos or on the camera for online class.\n\nPeople say they don’t notice it, but I still feel like everyone in the CNC lab sees that first, before anything I’m doing.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your face is actually crooked, or is it just that you feel like it looks that way to others?\n\nPatient: I mean… I think it is crooked. Even when people say they don’t see it, I still feel like they do—like people in the lab notice that before anything else.\n\nDoctor: Is it only your face that you're concerned about, or are there other parts of your body that you worry about as well?\n\nPatient: It’s mostly my face. The left side, specifically… like my nose and jaw look crooked to me. I notice it in ID photos or on the camera for class, and then I can’t really stop thinking about it. Even in the CNC lab, I feel like people see that before they see my work.\n\nDoctor: Do you think it's possible that your face might actually look normal, but you just can't see it that way?\n\nPatient: I… I guess it’s possible. People keep saying they don’t see it. But when I look at ID photos or the class camera, the left side just looks wrong to me—like my nose and jaw are crooked. And then in the CNC lab I feel like that’s what everyone notices first.\n\nDoctor: Do you think it's possible for you to be mistaken about your face being crooked?\n\nPatient: I mean… maybe. People tell me they don’t see it, so I guess I could be wrong. But when I see my ID photo or the online class camera, the left side just looks off—like my nose and jaw are crooked. It’s hard to believe everyone in the CNC lab isn’t noticing that before my work.\n\nDoctor: Has your concern about the left side of your face looking crooked changed in intensity over time?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it’s gotten worse, I think. At first I’d just notice it in pictures sometimes, but now it’s like I can’t stop thinking about the left side of my face looking off. The ID photos, the class camera… it all makes my nose and jaw look crooked to me, and then I keep thinking people in the CNC lab are seeing that before anything else.\n\nDoctor: Do you think there's any chance that your belief about the left side of your face being crooked might not be based in reality?\n\nPatient: I guess there’s a chance… because people keep telling me they don’t see it. But I came in because I can’t stop thinking about it. In every ID photo or on the online class camera, the left side of my face looks off to me—my nose and jaw look crooked. And even when someone says it looks normal, I still feel like people in the CNC lab notice that before they notice my work.\n\nDoctor: Is your concern about the left side of your face affecting your ability to do things you want or need to do?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it is. I’ve missed some CNC labs and shop shifts because I just couldn’t make myself leave. My faculty mentor said my work placement might be in trouble if it keeps happening.\n\nAnd… I stopped playing soccer and volunteering. I just don’t want people looking at me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think other people can see the crookedness in your face, even if they don't say anything about it?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. Even if they don’t say anything, I feel like they can see it. Like in the CNC lab, I keep thinking they notice my face being off before they notice anything I’m doing.\n\nDoctor: Do you think it's possible that others are intentionally not telling you about your face being crooked, even if they've noticed it?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think that sometimes. I just see the left side—my nose, my jaw—looking crooked, and then I can’t stop thinking everyone in the CNC lab notices that before anything I’m doing.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your belief about your face being crooked would change if you were shown a professional, neutral assessment of your appearance, such as from a plastic surgeon?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… maybe for a little bit, but I think I’d still keep seeing it.\n\nLike, even when people tell me they don’t notice anything, I look at my ID photo or the camera for class and the left side just looks wrong to me—my nose, my jaw. Then I start thinking everyone in the CNC lab sees that before they see my work.\n\nDoctor: Do you think there's anything that could convince you that your face isn't actually crooked?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know. Maybe if I could stop seeing it in photos or on camera, but… I came in because I can’t stop thinking the left side of my face looks off. My nose, my jaw—especially in my ID photo or online class camera.\n\nEven when people say they don’t see it, I still feel like everyone in the CNC lab notices that first, before my work. So it’s hard to imagine what would convince me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your belief about your face being crooked is based on your own perception or on something that you think is objectively true?\n\nPatient: I guess it’s mostly my own perception… but it feels objective when I’m looking at it.\n\nLike in my ID photo or on the class camera, the left side of my face just looks off—my nose and jaw. Even if people say they don’t see it, I still feel like everyone in the CNC lab notices it before anything else.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your belief about your face being crooked is something that might be a mistaken idea that you have, or do you think it's a fact that you're certain of?\n\nPatient: I mean… part of me knows it could be mistaken, I guess. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Um… I guess I’m here because I can’t stop thinking about my face. Mostly the left side. It looks… off to me, like my nose and jaw are crooked, especially in ID photos or on the camera for online class.\n\nPeople say they don’t notice it, but I still feel like everyone in the CNC lab sees that first, before anything I’m doing.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your face is actually crooked, or is it just that you feel like it looks that way to others?\n\nPatient: I mean… I think it is crooked. Or at least it looks that way to me. The left side just looks off, like my nose and jaw don’t line up right, especially in photos or on camera. Even when people say they don’t see it, I still feel like they do—like people in the lab notice that before anything else.\n\nDoctor: Is it only your face that you're concerned about, or are there other parts of your body that you worry about as well?\n\nPatient: It’s mostly my face. The left side, specifically… like my nose and jaw look crooked to me. I notice it in ID photos or on the camera for class, and then I can’t really stop thinking about it. Even in the CNC lab, I feel like people see that before they see my work.\n\nDoctor: Do you think it's possible that your face might actually look normal, but you just can't see it that way?\n\nPatient: I… I guess it’s possible. People keep saying they don’t see it. But when I look at ID photos or the class camera, the left side just looks wrong to me—like my nose and jaw are crooked. And then in the CNC lab I feel like that’s what everyone notices first.\n\nDoctor: Do you think it's possible for you to be mistaken about your face being crooked?\n\nPatient: I mean… maybe. People tell me they don’t see it, so I guess I could be wrong. But when I see my ID photo or the online class camera, the left side just looks off—like my nose and jaw are crooked. It’s hard to believe everyone in the CNC lab isn’t noticing that before my work.\n\nDoctor: Has your concern about the left side of your face looking crooked changed in intensity over time?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it’s gotten worse, I think. At first I’d just notice it in pictures sometimes, but now it’s like I can’t stop thinking about the left side of my face looking off. The ID photos, the class camera… it all makes my nose and jaw look crooked to me, and then I keep thinking people in the CNC lab are seeing that before anything else.\n\nDoctor: Do you think there's any chance that your belief about the left side of your face being crooked might not be based in reality?\n\nPatient: I guess there’s a chance… because people keep telling me they don’t see it. But I came in because I can’t stop thinking about it. In every ID photo or on the online class camera, the left side of my face looks off to me—my nose and jaw look crooked. And even when someone says it looks normal, I still feel like people in the CNC lab notice that before they notice my work.\n\nDoctor: Is your concern about the left side of your face affecting your ability to do things you want or need to do?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it is. I’ve missed some CNC labs and shop shifts because I just couldn’t make myself leave. My faculty mentor said my work placement might be in trouble if it keeps happening.\n\nAnd… I stopped playing soccer and volunteering. I just don’t want people looking at me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think other people can see the crookedness in your face, even if they don't say anything about it?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. Even if they don’t say anything, I feel like they can see it. Like in the CNC lab, I keep thinking they notice my face being off before they notice anything I’m doing.\n\nDoctor: Do you think it's possible that others are intentionally not telling you about your face being crooked, even if they've noticed it?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think that sometimes. Like maybe they notice it but don’t want to be rude or make me feel worse. Even when people say they don’t see it, I still can’t stop thinking the left side looks off—my nose, my jaw… especially in photos or on camera. And in the CNC lab, it feels like they’re seeing that before anything else.\n\nDoctor: Do you think it's possible that your belief about your face being crooked is not true, even if it feels that way to you?\n\nPatient: I mean… I guess it’s possible. People keep saying they don’t see it.\n\nBut it doesn’t feel possible when I’m looking at a photo or my camera for class. I just see the left side—my nose, my jaw—looking crooked, and then I can’t stop thinking everyone in the CNC lab notices that before anything I’m doing.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your belief about your face being crooked would change if you were shown a professional, neutral assessment of your appearance, such as from a plastic surgeon?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… maybe for a little bit, but I think I’d still keep seeing it.\n\nLike, even when people tell me they don’t notice anything, I look at my ID photo or the camera for class and the left side just looks wrong to me—my nose, my jaw. Then I start thinking everyone in the CNC lab sees that before they see my work.\n\nDoctor: Do you think there's anything that could convince you that your face isn't actually crooked?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know. Maybe if I could stop seeing it in photos or on camera, but… I came in because I can’t stop thinking the left side of my face looks off. My nose, my jaw—especially in my ID photo or online class camera.\n\nEven when people say they don’t see it, I still feel like everyone in the CNC lab notices that first, before my work. So it’s hard to imagine what would convince me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your belief about your face being crooked is based on your own perception or on something that you think is objectively true?\n\nPatient: I guess it’s mostly my own perception… but it feels objective when I’m looking at it.\n\nLike in my ID photo or on the class camera, the left side of my face just looks off—my nose and jaw. Even if people say they don’t see it, I still feel like everyone in the CNC lab notices it before anything else.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your belief about your face being crooked is something that might be a mistaken idea that you have, or do you think it's a fact that you're certain of?\n\nPatient: I mean… part of me knows it could be mistaken, I guess. But when I see it in my ID photo or on the class camera, it feels like a fact. The left side just looks off—my nose and jaw—and I can’t stop focusing on it. Even if people say they don’t see it, I still feel like everyone in the CNC lab notices that before my work.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your belief about your face being crooked is something that could be completely false, with no basis in reality?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know. I can say maybe it could be false, but it doesn’t feel that way when I see myself. In every ID photo or on the class camera, the left side of my face looks off to me—my nose, my jaw. And even if people say they don’t notice, I still feel like people in the CNC lab see that before anything else.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. I’ve missed some CNC labs and shop shifts because I just couldn’t make myself leave. My faculty mentor said my work placement might be in trouble if it keeps happening.\n\nAnd… I stopped playing soccer and volunteering. I just don’t want people looking at me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think other people can see the crookedness in your face, even if they don't say anything about it?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. Even if they don’t say anything, I feel like they can see it. Like in the CNC lab, I keep thinking they notice my face being off before they notice anything I’m doing.\n\nDoctor: Do you think it's possible that others are intentionally not telling you about your face being crooked, even if they've noticed it?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think that sometimes. Like maybe they notice it but don’t want to be rude or make me feel worse. Even when people say they don’t see it, I still can’t stop thinking the left side looks off—my nose, my jaw… especially in photos or on camera. And in the CNC lab, it feels like they’re seeing that before anything else.\n\nDoctor: Do you think it's possible that your belief about your face being crooked is not true, even if it feels that way to you?\n\nPatient: I mean… I guess it’s possible. People keep saying they don’t see it.\n\nBut it doesn’t feel possible when I’m looking at a photo or my camera for class. I just see the left side—my nose, my jaw—looking crooked, and then I can’t stop thinking everyone in the CNC lab notices that before anything I’m doing.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your belief about your face being crooked would change if you were shown a professional, neutral assessment of your appearance, such as from a plastic surgeon?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… maybe for a little bit, but I think I’d still keep seeing it.\n\nLike, even when people tell me they don’t notice anything, I look at my ID photo or the camera for class and the left side just looks wrong to me—my nose, my jaw. Then I start thinking everyone in the CNC lab sees that before they see my work.\n\nDoctor: Do you think there's anything that could convince you that your face isn't actually crooked?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know. Maybe if I could stop seeing it in photos or on camera, but… I came in because I can’t stop thinking the left side of my face looks off. My nose, my jaw—especially in my ID photo or online class camera.\n\nEven when people say they don’t see it, I still feel like everyone in the CNC lab notices that first, before my work. So it’s hard to imagine what would convince me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your belief about your face being crooked is based on your own perception or on something that you think is objectively true?\n\nPatient: I guess it’s mostly my own perception… but it feels objective when I’m looking at it.\n\nLike in my ID photo or on the class camera, the left side of my face just looks off—my nose and jaw. Even if people say they don’t see it, I still feel like everyone in the CNC lab notices it before anything else.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your belief about your face being crooked is something that might be a mistaken idea that you have, or do you think it's a fact that you're certain of?\n\nPatient: I mean… part of me knows it could be mistaken, I guess. Even if people say they don’t see it, I still feel like everyone in the CNC lab notices that before my work.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your belief about your face being crooked is something that could be completely false, with no basis in reality?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know. Even if people say they don’t see it, I still feel like everyone in the CNC lab notices it before anything else.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your belief about your face being crooked is something that might be a mistaken idea that you have, or do you think it's a fact that you're certain of?\n\nPatient: I mean… part of me knows it could be mistaken, I guess. Even if people say they don’t see it, I still feel like everyone in the CNC lab notices that before my work.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your belief about your face being crooked is something that could be completely false, with no basis in reality?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know. Even if people say they don’t see it, I still feel like everyone in the CNC lab notices it before anything else.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your belief about your face being crooked is something that might be a mistaken idea that you have, or do you think it's a fact that you're certain of?\n\nPatient: I mean… part of me knows it could be mistaken, I guess. Even if people say they don’t see it, I still feel like everyone in the CNC lab notices that before my work.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your belief about your face being crooked is something that could be completely false, with no basis in reality?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know. And even if people say they don’t notice, I still feel like people in the CNC lab see that before anything else.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your belief about your face being crooked would change if everyone you trust told you it's not true?\n\nPatient: Maybe a little… but I don’t think it would really go away.\n\nEven when people tell me they don’t see it, I still keep seeing it in photos or on camera. Like the left side—my nose and jaw—just looks wrong to me, and then I start thinking people in the lab must notice that first.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your belief about your face being crooked would change if you saw a video of yourself from someone else's perspective, showing that others don't react to your face the way you think they do?\n\nPatient: Maybe… seeing it that way might help for a minute.
